Equivariant valuations on convex functions
Authors: Georg C. Hofstätter, Jonas Knoerr
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
We classify all continuous valuations on the space of finite convex functions with values in the same space which are dually epi-translation-invariant and equi- resp. contravariant with respect to volume-preserving linear maps. We thereby identify the valuation-theoretic functional analogues of the difference body map and show that there does not exist a generalization of the projection body map in this setting. This non-existence result is shown to also hold true for valuations with values in the space of convex functions that are finite in a neighborhood of the origin.

Introduction

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}^n$$\end{document}Kn denote the set of all convex bodies in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Rn (compact, convex, non-empty subsets); throughout, we assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2. and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\cdot )^\perp $$\end{document}(·)⊥ denotes the orthogonal complement. Projection bodies were introduced by Minkowski at the beginning of the previous century and have since then proved essential in the study of projections of convex bodies (see, e.g., [1, 2] and the references therein). Notably, projection and difference bodies play a key role in the solution of the Rogers–Shephard problem [3–5], in central inequalities in convex geometry like Petty’s projection inequality [6], Zhang’s inequality [7] and the Rogers–Shephard inequality [8], and are the key objects in Petty’s conjectured inequality [6], which is still an open problem.

About twenty years ago, Ludwig [9, 10] revealed a new perspective on projection and difference bodies. In her seminal work, she characterized them by their compatibility with respect to volume-preserving linear maps and a valuation property:

Following Theorem 1.1, many fundamental geometric constructions including (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_p$$\end{document}Lp) intersection bodies, centroid bodies and affine surface area were characterized as equivariant valuations (see [11–18]).

In recent years, substantial efforts were made to transfer and generalize geometric concepts (for convex bodies) to a functional setting, e.g., to convex or log-concave functions, see [19–37]. While there are several natural candidates for a difference function map (examined, e.g., in [38]), there is so far no suitable candidate for a generalization of the projection body map. In this article, we contribute to the solution of this problem, taking the perspective of valuation theory inspired by Theorem 1.1. More concretely put, we aim to identify the correct valuation-theoretic analogues of difference and projection bodies on the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Conv}(\mathbb {R}^n, \mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Conv(Rn,R) of finite convex functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\mathbb {R}^n \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:Rn→R.

In this setting, a valuation on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Conv}(\mathbb {R}^n, \mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Conv(Rn,R) is a map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu : maximum resp. minimum. Such a valuation is called dually epi-translation-invariant if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu (f+\ell ) = \mu (f)$$\end{document}μ(f+ℓ)=μ(f) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in \textrm{Conv}(\mathbb {R}^n, \mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈Conv(Rn,R) and all affine maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell :\mathbb {R}^n\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ℓ:Rn→R. We refer to [22, 39–41] for the geometric interpretation of this notion and an interpretation in terms of translation invariant valuations on convex bodies. Continuity is defined with respect to the topology induced by epi-convergence of convex functions, which for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Conv}(\mathbb {R}^n,\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Conv(Rn,R) coincides with locally uniform as well as pointwise convergence.

Our first main result is a classification of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{SL}\,}}(n,\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}SL(n,R)-equivariant valuations on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Conv}(\mathbb {R}^n, \mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Conv(Rn,R), thereby determining the valuation-theoretic difference function maps. Throughout the article we will write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (f)[x]$$\end{document}Ψ(f)[x] for the value of the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (f) \in \textrm{Conv}(\mathbb {R}^n, \mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Ψ(f)∈Conv(Rn,R) at the point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}x∈Rn. Theorem A

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu \in \mathcal {M}_c^+(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}ν∈Mc+(R) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{\mathbb {R}\setminus \{0\}}|s|^{-1} d\nu (s) < \infty $$\end{document}∫R\{0}|s|-1dν(s)<∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{\mathbb {R}\setminus \{0\}}s^{-1} d\nu (s) = 0$$\end{document}∫R\{0}s-1dν(s)=0;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu \in \mathcal {M}_c^+(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}ν∈Mc+(R) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{\mathbb {R}\setminus \{0\}}|s|^{-1} d\nu (s) < \infty $$\end{document}∫R\{0}|s|-1dν(s)<∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{\mathbb {R}\setminus \{0\}}s^{-1} d\nu (s) = 0$$\end{document}∫R\{0}s-1dν(s)=0;

Note that by setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c=0$$\end{document}c=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu = \delta _{-1} + \delta _1$$\end{document}ν=δ-1+δ1, we obtain the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (f)[x] =f(x) + f(-x) - 2f(0)$$\end{document}Ψ(f)[x]=f(x)+f(-x)-2f(0), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}x∈Rn, a dually epi-translation-invariant version of the map introduced and investigated as difference function map in [38]. In the proof of Theorem A, we make use of a construction introduced in [41], which allows us to associate a suitable distribution to any homogeneous valuation in this class, called Goodey–Weil distribution. The equivariance property then translates to an invariance property of the Goodey–Weil distribution, and we show that this implies that its support must be lower dimensional. The main technical result of this article establishes that valuations with this property can be obtained by a simple restriction procedure from valuations defined on the corresponding lower dimensional subspace (compare Theorem 3.1). In the setting of Theorem A, this allows us to reduce the problem to valuations which are homogeneous of degree 0 or 1 and thus to apply a previous result from [20] for additive endomorphisms.

In our second main result, we apply a similar strategy to characterize all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{SL}\,}}(n,\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}SL(n,R)-contravariant valuations, where a map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi :\textrm{Conv}(\mathbb {R}^n,\mathbb {R})\rightarrow \textrm{Conv}(\mathbb {R}^n,\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Ψ:Conv(Rn,R)→Conv(Rn,R) is called \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{SL}\,}}(n,\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}SL(n,R)-contravariant if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (f\circ g)=\Psi (f)\circ g^{-T}$$\end{document}Ψ(f∘g)=Ψ(f)∘g-T for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\in {{\,\textrm{SL}\,}}(n,\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}g∈SL(n,R) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in \textrm{Conv}(\mathbb {R}^n,\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}f∈Conv(Rn,R). Except for the special case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2$$\end{document}n=2, which can be deduced from Theorem A by composing with a rotation by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi /2$$\end{document}π/2, only trivial (that is, constant) valuations can appear here. Consequently, there is no direct analogue of the projection body map in this setting.
